● ECC 2.2.3 is installed as a plugin. It has 311 skills, 97 commands and 71 
  agents. Your ecc-workspace folder is empty, so there's no project here yet to
  set up.

  How the pieces fit:
  - Skills are the main way to use ECC. Each is a packaged workflow, like
    tdd-workflow, security-review or api-design. Claude can load them as needed,
    or you can run them as /ecc:<name>.
  - Commands are slash shortcuts that mostly start a skill or agent, like
    /ecc:plan or /ecc:code-review.
  - Agents are specialist helpers that tasks get handed to, like
    ecc:python-reviewer or ecc:build-error-resolver.
  - Hooks are safety checks that run automatically. You just saw one: the
    "Fact-Forcing Gate" (GateGuard) made me say what a shell command does before
    running it.

  Pick a starting point:
  1. Set up ECC for a project: open a repo and run /ecc:project-init. It works
     out your stack and shows a plan of what it would change before applying
     anything.
  2. Find a workflow for a task: tell me what you're trying to do (e.g. "add
     tests to a FastAPI app") and I'll name the best skill or agent.
  3. Learn the core loop: /ecc:plan → /ecc:tdd-workflow or /ecc:feature-dev →
     /ecc:code-review. COMMANDS-QUICK-REF.md in the plugin folder has the cheat
     sheet.
  4. Check hooks and safety: see hooks/README.md, or run /ecc:security-scan on
     your config.
  5. Check how well a repo is set up: /ecc:harness-audit.

  GateGuard will ask for that statement on the first shell command of each
  session. To turn just that check off, set GATEGUARD_BASH_ROUTINE_DISABLED=1.
  The checks for destructive commands stay on.

  Which one do you want, or what are you working on?

  # Context handoff: GPU project → SLO-aware resource governor                  
                                                                                
  You're continuing a project with me (Arjun, final-year B.Tech AI/ML,          
  targeting NVIDIA graduate roles). Read `CLAUDE.md` in this repo first. This   
  prompt is the full handoff. Where they differ, this prompt wins, so update    
  `CLAUDE.md` to match.                                                         
                                                                                
  ## How I want you to work                                                     
  - Be blunt and accurate. Depth over frameworks. Ask me one question at a      
  time, and only when blocked.                                                  
  - Never fabricate or estimate metrics as if measured. Every number in         
  docs/README must trace to a logged file.                                      
  - Verify hardware capabilities by running commands. Don't assume.             
  - Before claiming novelty or citing a paper, verify it exists.                
                                                                                
  ## Hardware and environment                                                   
  - AMD Ryzen 9 + NVIDIA RTX 4070, probably the Laptop variant (36 SMs, 8 GB,   
  ~256 GB/s), possibly desktop (46 SMs, 12 GB). Confirm first:                  
    `nvidia-smi --query-gpu=name,memory.total,power.limit,power.max_limit,clock 
  s.max.sm,clocks.max.mem --format=csv`                                         
  - Windows primary (WDDM; no MPS, no MIG on GeForce). A Linux dual-boot arm    
  (MPS, RAPL, sched_ext) is optional and comes later.                           
  - CUDA builds: always `nvcc -arch=sm_89 -O3 -lineinfo`. Never allow a silent  
  CPU-simulation fallback: fail loudly and log the execution mode in every      
  results file.                                                                 
                                                                                
  ## Part 1: The old project (archive it, don't extend it)                      
  `variance_gpu_project` studied "variance-aware adaptive chunking": a host     
  loop launched one kernel per chunk, synced after each one, and shrank chunks  
  when the variance of recent task costs was high. v1 is in `src/`, v2 in       
  `experiments/variance_adaptive_v2/`.                                          
                                                                                
  Established findings, from re-analysing the v2 traces                         
  (`experiments/variance_adaptive_v2/analysis/barrier_model.py`):               
  - Adaptive lost to static-large and static-matched in experiments B, C and D. 
  The README blames launch overhead. **That's wrong**: host overhead            
  (t_total_chunk − t_kernel) is only 5–34 ms per run, and the gap of several    
  seconds is inside kernel time.                                                
  - Correct mechanism: Graham's list-scheduling bound. T_total ≈ W/S + Σᵢ Lᵢ,   
  where Lᵢ = the slowest warp in chunk i. The fit a + b·max_cost + c·Σwarp_max  
  gives R² ≈ 0.97, and per-arm totals land within ~5%. Every chunk boundary is  
  a barrier that pays the max again. Adaptive puts extra barriers exactly in    
  the high-variance regions, which is why it loses to static-matched at the     
  same chunk count.                                                             
  - Classic dynamic loop self-scheduling theory (Kruskal & Weiss 1985;          
  factoring, Hummel et al. 1992; adapti                                         
  ──── (63 lines hidden) ────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  mes. Fix experimental conditions (AC power, fixed fan mode, log ambient       
  temperature, warm-up).                                                        
  - **W2–3 Characterisation:** grid of foreground × background × knobs.         
  Validate the residual-wait model against measured kernel durations, and       
  validate the decode downclocking claim. Use the v2 method.                    
  - **W4–5 MVP:** telemetry daemon + controllable background runtime +          
  foreground harness + greedy/static/PI controllers.                            
  - **W6–8 Learned controller:** compare against PI and the oracle.             
  - **W9–10 Real scenarios:** (a) game built-in benchmark + local LLM agent;    
  (b) chatbot or captions + image generation. Battery mode, ablations, open     
  dataset and harness release, 6–8 page report.                                 
                                                                                
  Foreground workloads, in order: synthetic deadline-driven CUDA/graphics frame 
  loop (reproducible), then AI foreground with an SLO (llama.cpp chat           
  TTFT/TPOT, or Whisper captions), then a real game benchmark on Windows.       
                                                                                
  ### Suggested new repo layout (new repo, sibling folder `governor/`)          
  ```                                                                           
  governor/                                                                     
    CLAUDE.md                                                                   
    telemetry/      # samplers: nvml, presentmon, cpu_power, pdh; synchronised  
  timestamped logging                                                           
    actuators/      # clocks, cpu (priority/affinity/ecoqos/ryzenadj),          
  background app control API                                                    
    workloads/                                                                  
      background/   # sliced CUDA job (port of CudaExecutor), llama.cpp wrapper 
      foreground/   # synthetic frame loop with deadlines, chat/caption SLO     
  client                                                                        
    controllers/    # greedy, static, pi, learned (model + mpc/bandit), safety  
  guard                                                                         
    experiments/    # pre-registered configs (yaml), runner with randomised     
  order + warm-up                                                               
    analysis/       # stats (paired Wilcoxon, effect sizes), plots, model       
  validation                                                                    
    data/           # raw traces (gitignored if large) + manifest               
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## Your first tasks (in order)                                                
  1. Archive the old project (Part 1).                                          
  2. Confirm the GPU model and record the full specs in `CLAUDE.md`.            
  3. Create `governor/` with the layout above and a `CLAUDE.md` carrying this   
  context.                                                                      
  4. Write `governor/telemetry/capability_audit.py`. For each sensor and        
  actuator above, it reports: available (Y/N), needs admin, sampling or         
  actuation latency, and a sample reading or measured effect. Include:          
     - an NVML sampling-rate test                                               
     - a clock-lock apply/revert test with before/after clocks under a small    
  CUDA load                                                                     
     - PresentMon detection                                                     
     - Ryzen power source detection                                             
     - a VRAM headroom check                                                    
     Output to `governor/data/capability_audit.json` and a Markdown table.      
  5. Stop and show me the audit table before building anything else. Ask me one 
  question if a decision is needed.

  The cuda_executor.* edits are purely additive. They add two methods for a
  "SORTING-ADAPTIVE" arm:
  - launch_chunk_sorted() sorts the next chunk on the CPU, re-uploads that slice
    and launches the kernel.
  - reset_device_costs() restores the original task order between repetitions.

  They are consistent with sorting_adaptive_scheduler.h, which is untracked and
  looks like part of the same change. Nothing in the diff touches the silent
  simulation fallback or the -arch flag. The comments contain unmeasured claims,
  such as "~0.1ms" for the sort and "~4 microseconds" for the PCIe transfer.

  Git also warns that the files' line endings will switch from LF to CRLF the
  next time Git touches them.

  Decision for you: are these edits yours? Either way I'd commit them in the
  archive snapshot, so the history shows exactly what the tree held. If they
  aren't yours, I'd make them a separate commit from the files you wrote, so
  they're easy to revert.

  I can commit them all together, commit the unrecognised edits separately, or
  leave cuda_executor.* uncommitted.

       (fe6eb72), before any data. It covers 2 kernels × 6 clock settings (600,
       900, 1200, 1500, 1800 MHz and unlocked), 10 randomised blocks, and
       Holm-corrected paired Wilcoxon tests.
     - My unlocked smoke run gave 232 GB/s on the streaming kernel (about 90% of
       the 256 GB/s from your handoff) and 18.7 TFLOP/s on the FMA kernel
       (about 82% of theoretical peak). Both numbers come from that short smoke
       run, not the experiment.
     - The analysis code ran correctly on synthetic data. That data lives only
       in my scratch folder and none of it is in the repo.

  What to run (Administrator PowerShell, laptop on AC, nothing else using the 
  GPU)

  cd %USERPROFILE%\governor
  python telemetry\energy_meter_validation.py
  python experiments\w1_downclock\run.py --ambient-c <room temp in C>
  python analysis\w1_downclock_analysis.py
  - Energy Meter script (~2.5 min): have HWiNFO's Sensors window open. When the
    script prints >>> READ HWiNFO, note CPU Package Power for the load phase and
    again for the idle phase. Then re-run python 
    telemetry\energy_meter_validation.py --from-csv <the csv it printed> 
    --hwinfo-load <W> --hwinfo-idle <W> for the comparison.
  - Downclock run (~20 min): it needs the same Administrator shell. It restores
    clocks even if it crashes.

  The 94 W vs 28.5 W numbers

  - 28.5 W is a single NVML power read at an SM clock locked to 1200 MHz, from
    the elevated audit run, under the 4096×4096 fp16 matmul load.
  - 94.2 W is a single NVML read at 2415 MHz from the earlier non-admin audit
    run (commit 7120171), under the same load. The elevated run's own unlocked
    read was 95.2 W.
  - These are one-off reads from different runs, not a matched comparison. The
    table the downclock run produces is the matched one.

  Open risk: in the smoke run the sampled power lagged the energy counter (59 W
  against 89 W). The harness flags that check, and the 1.5 s pre-roll should
  help. If many rows get flagged in the full run, I'll say so and treat the
  energy counter as the primary source.

  and idle watts when you have them.

● Now the plot you asked to see.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● The plot confirms cause (b) — power signals update in stair-steps every
  ~500ms-1s with ~1.5s settling time — while ruling out cause (c), since instant
  and average readings match; it also reveals a new issue where the energy
  counter inflates ~4.2J per extra read. Next, I'll check finding 3 by reading
  the raw mask under load and comparing it against nvidia-smi's output at the
  same moment. · summary

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed via nvidia-smi: the throttle under fma load is a real "Reliability"
  limit (0x400), not the SW power cap, which contradicts the pre-registered
  limitation text—I'll note this in the addendum and harden the decoder against
  fragile attribute-name scanning. Next, I'll write failing TDD tests for
  findings 2, 3, and 4 before implementing any fixes. · summary

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed RED: both test modules fail since nvml_utils and provenance aren't
  implemented yet. Now checking how the existing decoder handles an unknown bit
  for extra RED-stage evidence. · summary

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The current decoder silently drops unknown bits, so values like 0x800 return
  "None" and 0x804 just "SwPowerCap," masking real throttle states. I'll now
  write a minimal implementation that fixes this. · summary

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The tests are green (16 passed). Now I'm wiring the helpers into run.py and
  the audit script, so the fixes apply to the code that actually runs.

● Findings 1–4 are fixed and committed, the code review is done and answered,
  and the tree is clean. The downclock run is still waiting on you, and there is
  one decision for you first, below.

  Finding 1: the energy-counter mismatch has two causes, and I found one the 
  review didn't list.
  - Power samples lag. All three NVML power signals update every ~0.5 s and take
    ~1.5 s to follow a load step. The smoke-run mismatch came from 0.5 s
    pre-rolls and 1 s windows that never reached steady state. At the registered
    1.5 s pre-roll and 4 s window, the samples read about 11% below the counter
    at ~80 W. That is just inside the 15% check.
  - The energy counter is biased.
    - Each extra read adds about 4.2 J of phantom energy. My first diagnostic
      read it at 100 Hz and was wrong, so I rebuilt it.
    - The counter also sits above the sampled power by an amount that depends on
      GPU state: idle 22–28 W against 2.4 W, fma 82–86 W against 77–80 W.
  - Energy-per-work ratios across clock levels may therefore be biased. The
    addendum experiments/w1_downclock_addendum_2026-10-20.md records this, and
    the registered yaml is untouched. I added one pre-specified check: H2 is
    re-tested using the sampled power. If the two energy sources disagree on the
    decision or on which levels are significant, H2 is reported as
    inconclusive.
  - The plot is at data/diagnostics/power_energy_20261020T191306Z.png.

  Findings 2–4 were done test-first (RED commit 46b995a, GREEN commit c2f51e3,
  16 tests passing):
  - Finding 2. temp_start_c was a reading taken before the pre-roll, not a stale
    value. It's now the first sample in the measured window. The gate reading
    is kept as temp_pre_c, and the pre-roll heating is visible (59 °C before, 71
    °C at window start).
  - Finding 3. "Reliability" is correct. nvidia-smi independently reports
    "Reliability: Active" (raw 0x400) under fma load. The real bug was that
    unknown bits were silently dropped and shown as "None". The decoder is now a
    tested table that reports Unknown_0x… for bits it doesn't know. One side
    effect: unlocked fma is limited by Reliability, not by the power cap, which
    contradicts a line in the registered yaml. The addendum notes this.
  - Finding 4. run.py now refuses a full run on a dirty git tree, which also
    covers untracked files. The energy-meter logs are committed.

  The code review raised 10 findings. I fixed 7, disputed 2 with reasons, and
  partly accepted one. The full dispositions are in
  reviews/2026-10-20_w1_response.md.

  Decision for you: keep the registered 1.5 s pre-roll, or move to 3 s? Both are
  listed in the response file.
  - Keep it and run now.
  - Use 3 s. This makes the sample-based check informative. It needs a new
    w1_downclock_v2.yaml registered before you run, and I'd write it first.

  Once you've chosen, run this in an Administrator PowerShell, with 24 replaced
  by your real room temperature in °C, or the flag dropped:
  cd %USERPROFILE%\governor
  python experiments\w1_downclock\run.py --ambient-c 24

  While it runs (~20 min):
  - Leave the GPU alone. Don't run games, browsers with video, or the CPU
    energy-meter script. Any GPU activity puts the clock-lock and energy
    readings out of spec.
  - Expect some energy_vs_samples flags. They come from the counter offset and
    the sample lag. They're logged and the cells stay in the data. A
    clock_off_target or start_temp_not_reached flag is the kind that needs a
    look.
  - Check that it ends cleanly. The last line should be done in … min, and the
    harness resets the clocks on exit. If it dies midway, run nvidia-smi -rgc
    and nvidia-smi -rmc in the admin shell.

  Throughput relative to unlocked (median over 10 blocks)

  ┌────────────────┬───────┬───────┐
  │ SM clock (MHz) │  mem  │  fma  │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ 600            │ 0.809 │ 0.244 │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ 900            │ 0.889 │ 0.366 │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ 1200           │ 0.993 │ 0.488 │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ 1500           │ 0.997 │ 0.609 │
  ├────────────────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ 1800           │ 0.998 │ 0.732 │
  └────────────────┴───────┴───────┘

  Unlocked ran at about 2475 MHz for mem and 2460 for fma.

  - H1 (supported, 5 of 5 levels): the memory-bound kernel keeps more throughput
    than the compute-bound one. Holm-adjusted p was 0.0049 at every level, and
    the paired differences all had the same sign.
  - H2 (supported, 5 of 5 levels): memory-bound energy per GB falls below
    unlocked at every locked level. The best is 0.649 at 1200 MHz, and the
    median is 0.737 at 600 MHz.
  - Both energy sources agree. The sample-based re-test gives the same decision
    and the same significant levels, so H2 is not downgraded to inconclusive.
    The counter bias I flagged earlier does not change the conclusion here.

  What the data doesn't show, so you don't over-read it:
  - A 1200 MHz lock costs mem almost nothing. It keeps 99.3% of throughput at 37
    W against 57.7 W unlocked. Part of that gap is the unlocked clock sitting
    about 1000 MHz higher, and I haven't separated that from voltage and power
    effects.
  - The energy advantage is mostly not specific to memory-bound work. The
    exploratory comparison of relative energy per work, mem against fma, is
    significant only at 600 and 900 MHz. At 1200 MHz and above it is not
    significant (Holm p ≥ 0.13). fma also drops to 0.68 relative energy per work
    at 1200 MHz. The two kernels differ in throughput loss, but not much in
    energy efficiency at mid-to-high clocks.
  - fma energy per work is U-shaped. It reaches 0.995 at 600 MHz because static
    power dominates when the work rate is that low.
  - Seven of the 120 cells carry an energy_vs_samples flag. They are mostly fma
    at 600 and 900 MHz. The cells stay in the data, and the sample-based test
    agrees with the counter-based one anyway.
  - No clock-target, start-temperature or checksum failures. There is one
    distinct checksum per kernel.
  - This is one laptop and two synthetic kernels. It says nothing yet about a
    real foreground game load.

  The old audit numbers (94 W unlocked against 28.5 W locked) were single reads
  from different runs, so I wouldn't use them. The matched numbers above replace
  them.

  W2 is next, on the residual-wait model and the foreground/background grid. Do
  you want me to start it, or look at anything in these results first?

● Plan: W2, co-location characterisation (pre-registered)

  Journal 0006's design implication is built in: the SM clock lock is
  device-wide, so Experiment B measures what it does to a compute-bound
  foreground. I committed the research record as 75c7e42, including a new
  journal entry, 0007. It records the corrections found in the fact-check.
  Nothing is rewritten.

  Fact-check corrections (0007)

  - Journal 0006: "fma ~0.68–0.70 at ≥1200 MHz" is wrong at 1800 MHz, where fma
    is 0.762 and mem is 0.767. The mem−fma difference is significant only at 600
    and 900 MHz.
  - Journal 0003: the host overhead is 2–34 ms per run, not 5–34 ms. The
    warp-efficiency (0.46 / 0.19) and 14%-occupancy figures are unverified.
    ARCHIVE_NOTE itself says to re-verify them before citing, so don't cite them
    in a paper.
  - Journal 0002: the 0.75 ms launch overhead comes only from a doc string. I
    found no logged measurement.
  - Journal 0004: the paper (arXiv 2506.17538) exists. Its "30× slower" is
    Whisper/LiveCaptions decode on an RTX 6000 server, not LLM decode. The paper
    calls for SLO-aware scheduling but doesn't list it as future work.
  - Unverified: I did not open the G-Assist news claim.
  - Knee: the "~1200 MHz knee" in 0006 is only bracketed, between 900 and 1200
    MHz. So C below uses a finer grid.

  Requirements

  - A. Residual-wait model. Test whether foreground latency follows the
    non-preemptive M/G/1 prediction, wait ≈ E[S²]/(2E[S]) = m(1+c²)/2 for slice
    mean m and slice coefficient of variation c. The test uses realised slice
    times and Poisson foreground arrivals.
  - B. Device-wide clock lock. Measure what the lock does to a compute-bound
    foreground, plus a memory-bound foreground as a control. This includes
    foreground latency, deadline misses, and GPU power.
  - C. Decode-like background. Measure its clock knee and energy optimum on a
    finer grid, instead of assuming 1200 MHz.
  - Everything is pre-registered in one committed yaml before any data. The
    rules are the same as W1. Full runs need a clean tree, admin, a logged
    execution mode, no CPU fallback, and a thermal gate. Every number must trace
    to a logged file.

  What I expect to be hard (W1 didn't test any of this)

  - Windows has no MPS. Two processes share the GPU by time-slicing contexts,
    and Ada supports compute preemption. So the "non-preemptive kernel"
    assumption may be false, and the model failing would be a real result. The
    design separates three outcomes:
    - Linear in slice length: the model holds.
    - Saturating: the OS scheduler caps the wait.
    - Near zero: the kernels ran concurrently.
  - Arrival timing. The default Windows timer tick is 15.6 ms, so the foreground
    arrival loop needs QPC spin-waiting on a pinned core.
  - Coordinated omission. Latency is measured from the scheduled arrival time. A
    request that queues behind a late one is counted, not skipped.

  Patterns to mirror

  Source: experiments/w1_downclock/run.py
  Pattern: yaml-driven blocks with seeded randomisation, thermal gate, Sampler,
  provenance gate, CSV with flags, meta.json

  Source: workloads/background/bench_kernels.cu + build.bat

  Design

  A: Residual-wait test

  - Background (separate process). It runs a compute-bound slice kernel back to
    back with no gap, syncing after each slice. The grid fills all 36 SMs. Slice
    durations are drawn from a seeded gamma distribution with the target mean m
    and CV c. The background logs every realised slice with cudaEvent and QPC
    times.
  - Foreground (separate process, pinned core).
    - A small compute kernel of about 0.1 ms in isolation, arriving as a Poisson
      process at 200 requests/s on an absolute QPC time grid.
    - 2000 requests per cell.
    - Latency runs from scheduled arrival to the host seeing completion.
    - QPC is system-wide, so the two logs share a clock.
  - Factors.

  ┌────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │ Factor │        Levels         │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ m (ms) │ 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8 │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ c      │ 0, 0.5, 1.0           │
  └────────┴───────────────────────┘

    That is 18 cells, plus an isolated-foreground baseline cell per block for
  the service floor. There are 5 blocks in random order, 10 s warm-up, and the
  thermal gate from W1. The clock is unlocked, and the realised SM clock is
  logged.
  - Prediction. For each cell, R = E[S²]/(2E[S]) from the realised slice times,
    and predicted wait = R + baseline latency.

  Hypotheses, written before any data:
  - HA1. The median-over-blocks wait lies within ±25% of R in at least 70% of
    cells, and a regression of wait on R (blocks bootstrapped) has a slope CI
    that includes 1.
    - If it fails, the failure is classified. A wait that stops growing with m
      (the second-half slope is under half the first-half slope) is
      scheduler-capped. A wait under 20% of R at m ≥ 2 ms is concurrent or 
      preempted.
  - HA2. At fixed m, wait grows with c. The model predicts ratios of 1.25 at c =
    0.5 and 2.0 at c = 1 against c = 0. Tested with a one-sided paired Wilcoxon
    at each m, Holm over the 6 values of m.
  - Reported, not tested: p99 and p99.9 against the longest observed slice.
  - Optional, cut first if time runs short: A2, the same grid in one process
    with two streams (what the old executor does), on a 4-cell subset.

  B: Device-wide clock lock vs foreground

  - Foreground kinds. A compute-bound fma frame (about 2 ms of work, released at
    60 Hz) and a memory-bound copy frame (the control).
  - Background. None, or the memory-bound kernel running continuously in 1 ms
    slices.
  - SM clock levels. 1200, 1500, 1800, unlocked, plus the knee from C. If the
    knee is already one of those, that level is not repeated.
  - Replication. 10 blocks, 4 s window, same harness as W1.
  - Logged. Frame latency p50/p99/max, the share of frames over 16.7 ms,
    background throughput, and GPU energy from the counter and the samples.

  Hypotheses:
  - HB1. With the background off, the fma foreground's median service time at
    each locked level equals 1 / (W1's relative throughput for fma at that
    clock), within ±10%.
    - That prediction is 2.05× at 1200, 1.64× at 1500, and 1.37× at 1800.
    - The mem foreground is unchanged within ±5%.
  - HB2. The frame-deadline miss rate increases as the clock drops, for the fma
    foreground only. Tested with a one-sided paired Wilcoxon against unlocked,
    Holm over levels.
  - Report as net. The GPU power saved per level against the foreground slowdown
    it costs. It carries the W1 energy-bias caveat and the sample-based
    cross-check from the W1 addendum.

  C: Decode-like background and its knee

  - Choice (default). A synthetic transformer-shaped decode that I write myself
    (ADR-0005 covers own instrumentation). One token does about 32 layers of
    GEMV plus a KV-cache attention read over a resident weight set of about 1.5
    GB, with one host sync per token.
  - Variants. fp16 weights, and int4 weights with dequantisation (more ALU work
    per byte, so its knee should sit higher). The knee is measured separately
    for each.
  - SM clock levels. 600, 750, 900, 1050, 1200, 1350, 1500, 1800, unlocked. That
    is 2 variants × 9 levels × 10 blocks = 180 measurements.
  - Pre-registered definitions.
    - Knee: the lowest level whose median throughput is at least 95% of
      unlocked. A block-bootstrap CI is reported.
    - Energy optimum: the level with the lowest J per token.
    - Both are reported even if they differ.
  - Hypothesis HC1. The int4 variant's knee is higher than the fp16 variant's, a
    paired comparison across blocks. If the knee for both is at or below 1200
    MHz, that is reported as such.
  - Caveat stated up front. A synthetic proxy is not llama.cpp. After C, I'd run
    one real-engine cell only as a validation, and only if you choose it.

  Tasks

  1. Write the tests first (RED commit):
     - Slice generator hits the target mean and CV.
     - Arrival schedule is seeded and Poisson.
     - Latency counts a queued request instead of dropping it.
     - Residual-wait predictor matches hand-computed cases.
     - Knee rule picks the right level on known inputs.
     - Verify: python -m pytest tests -q shows them failing for the intended
       reason.
  2. Implement loadgen.py (GREEN). Verify: tests pass, coverage on the new
     module stays above 80%.
  3. Build the CUDA workloads with -arch=sm_89 -O3 -lineinfo. Smoke-run each one
     to check it executes on the GPU, with the execution mode logged.
  4. Pilot, allowed before registration. It checks three things: realised slice
     durations match their nominal values, timer overhead, and the isolated
     foreground latency. Pilot data goes in data/pilot/ and is never analysed as
     a result. It may set kernel iteration counts only, never thresholds or
     hypotheses.
  5. Write w2_colocation.yaml and commit it. Then the harness (smoke mode), then
     the analysis checked on synthetic data kept outside the repo, as in W1.
  6. Run C first. It gives the clock levels B needs. The commands are for you,
     from an Administrator shell as in W1.
  7. Run B and A, analyse, and write the journal entry. Show you the tables
     before W3.

      24  ## Why "high variance -> smaller chunks" inverted
      25  Kruskal & Weiss (1985), factoring (Hummel et al. 1992) and adap
          tive factoring (Banicescu) justify smaller chunks under varianc
          e with many independent workers and no global barrier. This har
          ness had p=1 plus a barrier per chunk, so the theory's premise 
          does not hold. (Citations are from memory of the literature, no
          t re-verified here.)

  w2_colocation.yaml, as you asked. Items 1 and 2 are done, and the tests and
  helpers for item 3 are done; the pilot results below need your decisions.

  Item 1: HAGS.
  - Journal 0010: dxdiag confirms the NVIDIA adapter has Hardware Scheduling: 
    DriverSupportState:Stable Enabled:True.
  - iGPU: the integrated AMD 780M reports "AlwaysOff", but it isn't the compute
    device.
  - meta.json: the pilot already parses the line from dxdiag /t. The harness
    will log it the same way.
  - Limitation: the HAGS-flip control is listed as a limitation, with a
    follow-up pre-registered only if Experiment A comes out scheduler-capped.

  Item 2: archive. ARCHIVE_NOTE.md now cites warp_efficiency.py (ba69c04) and

  Item 3: tests and helpers.

    covered.
  - Helpers: the slice generator, the arrival grid, latency from the scheduled
    arrival, residual-wait with ρ and gap stats, the knee rule, the
    slope-equivalence test, and the dxdiag parser.

  Pilot numbers. They come from data/pilot/w2/20261020T222839Z/pilot.json. The

  The decode rows are unlocked. The knee needs Experiment C. The fp16 proxy runs
  near the W1 streaming bandwidth. int4 reaches about 64% of it, so launch cost
  isn't what limits it.

  Two findings that change the design:
  1. Isolated foreground timing depends on the GPU's clock state. At light load
     the GPU sits near 1500 MHz, so a "1 ms" kernel takes about 1.6 ms. After an
     idle gap the first requests hit 210 MHz. The first 1 ms kernel took about
     25 ms, which backed up the queue for 170–370 ms
     (data/pilot/w2/diagnostics/stall_diag2_output.txt). It isn't noise. Every
     cell needs a warm-up that is excluded from the statistics.
  2. The one co-run cell looks preemption-like, not like a foreground waiting 
     out the background slice.
     - Setup: background slices of 2 ms, foreground arrivals at 200/s.
     - Prediction: the residual-wait model says about 1.07 ms of wait.
     - Observed: foreground latency p50 is 0.40 ms, and the kernel runs at full
       speed (0.086 ms).
     - Phase: latency barely depends on where in the slice the request arrives.
     - Slice stretch: background slices that contain a foreground completion run
       about 0.3 ms longer.
     - Replication: the two pilot runs agree.
     - Caveat: this is a single unregistered cell. It is not evidence for or
       against HA1, and I didn't tune anything to it.

  Decisions I need from you before I write the yaml:
  1. Run A at a fixed SM clock, with admin? I recommend this, because the
     baseline and the co-run cell need the same clock. Do you want 1800 MHz for
     A? Unlocked clock behaviour is exactly the confound described above.
  2. Add a pre-registered secondary: background slice stretch per foreground 
     request? If the preemption pattern holds, it would be the result worth
     reporting. HA1 stays as registered either way.
  3. Warm-up length per cell. I propose at least 1.5 s of foreground traffic,
     excluded from the statistics, matching W1's pre-roll. Do you want it
     longer?

  I haven't written the end-of-session journal entry yet. 0011 covers the pilot,
  and I'll add a closing entry when you end the session.

● The W2 pre-registration draft is written and parses. I stopped before
  committing, as you asked.

  The two uncommitted files are experiments/w2_colocation.yaml and
  journal/0012_2026-10-21_w2-decisions-after-pilot.md. Journal 0011 already held
  the pilot, the preemption surprise and why it matters, and it is committed.
  Journals are append-only, so your decisions and the design changes are in a
  new entry, 0012.

  Your four decisions are in the yaml:
  1. Fixed clocks for A. SM is locked at 1800 MHz and memory at 8001 MHz. Every
     W2 cell locks memory at 8001. The resets run in a finally block. A cell is
     flagged if any 20 Hz sample leaves ±3% SM or ±1% memory.
  2. HA1 unchanged. The secondary preemption characterisation is marked as
     motivated by the pilot:
     - SA1 phase independence: regress foreground latency on arrival phase. The
       slope is about −1 if the foreground waits out the slice and about 0 if it
       doesn't.
     - SA2 slice stretch: estimate the constant switch cost s0 and the stretch
       per millisecond of foreground service time k.
     - SA3 priority control: foreground normal vs raised priority on 4 cells.
     - SA4 foreground size and duty cycle: 0.1, 1, 4 and 9 ms foreground work,
       plus foreground slowdown against a swept background duty cycle.
  3. Warm-up. 2 s of foreground traffic per cell is excluded from all
     statistics.
  4. Realised slice durations are used in every prediction.

  Things I chose that you should check:
  - 10 blocks for A, not 5. With n paired blocks, the minimum Wilcoxon p is
    1/2ⁿ. At n = 5 that is 0.031, which could never pass Holm over six tests. At
    n = 10 it is 0.001.
  - HA1 supported only if both conditions hold: the 90% bootstrap CI of the
    slope lies inside [0.8, 1.25], and at least 70% of cells have observed wait
    within ±25% of predicted. I also pre-registered how a failure is classified:
    preempted/concurrent, scheduler-capped, or other.
  - HA2 supported if significant at 4 or more of the 6 slice sizes, after Holm.
  - Main-grid foreground priority is NORMAL. The pilot's co-run cell used a
    HIGH-priority foreground, so SA3 is the test of that difference.
  - Experiment B uses levels 900, 1200, 1500, 1800 and unlocked. The Experiment
    C knee is added by a dated addendum before B runs.
  - HB1 predicts foreground slowdown as 1 / (W1 relative throughput). The
    analysis reads those values from the W1 results file.
  - HB2 is supported only if the deadline-miss rate rises significantly at every
    level where HB1 puts the service time above the budget.
  - Experiment C has 36 cells per block. The int4 and fp16 variants and the
    stream-launch and CUDA-graph modes are crossed with 9 clock levels. The fp16
    variant has 14 layers because 28 don't fit next to the OS, and that count
    is logged.
  - llama.cpp validation is deferred to a dated addendum that must be written
    before it runs.

  Estimated run time is about 4.5 h total across the four parts, from the cell
  structure and not measured: A and the secondary about 1 h each, B about 1.3 h,
  C about 1 h. Total cells per block are 19 (A), 19 (secondary), 30 (B) and 36
  (C).

  Code still to write, test-first, before the first run:
  - a duty-cycle option in w2_kernels.cu (idle gap after each slice);
  - experiments/w2_colocation/run.py;
  - analysis/w2_analysis.py.

  The yaml lists these under implementation_prerequisites.

  Tell me what to change, or approve it and I'll commit it with journal 0012. No
  W2 measurement exists yet. The journal entry for this session is 0012; I'll
  add a closing one if the session ends with more done.

● Update(~\governor\journal\0014_2026-10-21_w2-experiment-c-results.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      25  2. The **int4 result is probably a property of my kernel, not o
          f Q4 decode.** The dequantisation in `gemv_q4` is scalar (nibbl
          e unpack, int→float, fma), with no dp4a/int8 paths, so it is AL
          U-bound and runs at ~64% of the bandwidth fp16 reaches. Real Q4
           engines use integer dot-product instructions and would sit bet
          ween the two proxies. HC1 therefore says "a dequantisation-heav
          y kernel has a higher knee", which is true but not a statement 
          about llama.cpp.
      26     This is the reason the registration defers the llama.cpp val
          idation cell; do not carry the int4 knee into governor claims b
          efore that cell runs.
      27  3. The fp16 proxy has half the layers (14 of 28) because 28 do 
          not fit next to the OS; the knee is a property of bytes per FLO
          P, not layer count, but this is unverified.
      28 -4. The energy optimum is at a lower clock than the knee for fp1
         -6 (750–900 vs 1050 MHz): a few percent of throughput buys a few
         - percent of energy.                                            
      28 +4. For fp16 the energy curve is flat between 750 and 1050 MHz (
         +graph mode 0.534 → 0.535 J/token while throughput falls from 0.
         +935 to 0.959-relative... i.e. 2.4 points), so the knee and the 
         +energy optimum differ by under 1% in energy; the 1050 MHz knee 
         +is the better operating point because it keeps the throughput. 
      29  ## Setbacks / surprises
      30  - A window power note: the unlocked cells run at ~2460–2475 MHz
           (Reliability-limited, 0.86 J/token for fp16), far from the eff
          icient region, consistent with W1.
      31  - The registered rule "append the C knee (int4 proxy, graph) to
           B's levels" is vacuous because that knee is `unlocked` (alread

      25  2. The **int4 result is probably a property of my kernel, not o
          f Q4 decode.** The dequantisation in `gemv_q4` is scalar (nibbl
          e unpack, int→float, fma), with no dp4a/int8 paths, so it is AL
          U-bound and runs at ~64% of the bandwidth fp16 reaches. Real Q4
           engines use integer dot-product instructions and would sit bet
          ween the two proxies. HC1 therefore says "a dequantisation-heav
          y kernel has a higher knee", which is true but not a statement 
          about llama.cpp.
      26     This is the reason the registration defers the llama.cpp val
          idation cell; do not carry the int4 knee into governor claims b
          efore that cell runs.
      27  3. The fp16 proxy has half the layers (14 of 28) because 28 do 
          not fit next to the OS; the knee is a property of bytes per FLO
          P, not layer count, but this is unverified.

         -energy optimum differ by under 1% in energy; the 1050 MHz knee 
         -is the better operating point because it keeps the throughput.

  Knees. The knee is the lowest SM clock that keeps at least 95% of the unlocked
  throughput. The knees were identical in all 10 blocks.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────┬─────────────────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │  proxy, graph    │   knee   │     energy optimum      │    J/token      │
  │       mode       │          │        (J/token)        │    unlocked     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ fp16, 14 layers  │ 1050 MHz │ 0.534 at 750 MHz        │ 0.861           │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ int4, 28 layers  │ unlocked │ 0.712 at 1500 MHz       │ 0.887           │
  └──────────────────┴──────────┴─────────────────────────┴─────────────────┘

  - HC1 is supported. The int4 knee is above the fp16 knee in 10 of 10 blocks (p
    = 0.00098, the minimum possible with 10 blocks). It compares "unlocked"
    with 1050 MHz.
  - The fp16 proxy is memory-bound over the whole range.
    - It keeps 91% of unlocked throughput at 600 MHz and 99% at 1800 MHz.
    - At 1050 MHz it keeps 0.96 of throughput and draws 43 W against 73 W
      unlocked.
    - Joules per token fall about 38% at the energy optimum. The energy curve is
      flat between 750 and 1050 MHz, so 1050 is the better operating point
      because it keeps the throughput.
    - These are board-level energy-counter figures, with the W1 caveats.
  - The knee is not 1200 MHz. For the memory-bound proxy it is 1050.
  - CUDA graphs barely matter. They add 1.8% tokens/s for fp16 and 4.8% for
    int4, and they moved no knee.

  Treat the int4 result with caution. The int4 proxy is clock-limited at every
  locked level: relative throughput is roughly proportional to the clock, 0.743
  at 1800 MHz for the graph mode. That is most likely my kernel, not Q4 decode.
  The dequantisation is scalar, with no integer dot-product path, so it is
  compute-bound and reaches only 64% of the bandwidth fp16 does. A real Q4
  engine would sit between the two proxies. I would not use the int4 knee in any
  governor claim until the llama.cpp validation cell has run.

  B-level addendum. The draft is
  experiments/w2_colocation_addendum_B_levels_2026-10-21.md, left uncommitted
  for your review.
  - Registered rule: it appends the int4 knee to B's levels, but that knee is
    "unlocked", which is already in the list. Read literally, nothing changes.
  - My proposal: add 1050 MHz as a descriptive level. HB1 and HB2 stay on
    900/1200/1500 against 1800, so the tests don't change.
  - Cost: it adds 6 cells per block (30 to 36) and roughly 16 minutes, estimated
    from the cell structure.
  - Alternative: keep B at five levels.
  - Commit first: the full-run guard refuses a dirty tree, so the addendum must
    be committed or removed before B runs.

  Decision for you: should B include 1050 MHz?

  If yes, I'll commit the addendum, update the B levels in design.py and its
  test first, then give you the Experiment B command.

● Nothing is running and no cells were completed (the partial folder
  data/raw/w2_B/20261021T104818Z has 0 rows). One thing is probably left over:
  the memory clock reads 8001 MHz at idle, which suggests the -lmc lock was not
  released when you interrupted. Resetting is harmless, and restarting is safe
  because there is no resume feature and a restart simply begins a fresh run.

  In your Administrator PowerShell, run:
  nvidia-smi -rgc
  nvidia-smi -rmc
  cd %USERPROFILE%\governor
  python experiments\w2_colocation\run.py --part B --ambient-c 25

  - Partial folder: leave it. It is gitignored, and the analysis automatically
    uses the newest run folder that has a results.csv, which will be the new
    one.
  - Clean tree: it is clean (0 changes) and AC is plugged in, so the run guards
    will pass.
  - If you interrupt again: press Ctrl+C once and wait a few seconds. The
    harness resets the clocks in a finally block, but only if Python is allowed
    to unwind. Closing the window or pressing Ctrl+C repeatedly can skip it, and
    the two reset commands above are the manual fix.
  - Resume: B takes about 2 hours and cannot resume mid-run. If you will need to
    stop partway, tell me, and I can add a block-level resume so a restart
    skips finished blocks, with a test first.

  Experiment B (c51b499, journal 0016): the run had 360 cells and no
  clock-excursion flags. The 220 fg_incomplete flags are all on cells with a
  miss fraction of 1.0 (overloaded), so I treated them as overload, not data
  loss.
  - HB1 is supported. A compute-bound foreground's service time scales with the
    clock: 1.999, 1.500 and 1.203 times the 1800 MHz time at 900, 1200 and 1500
    MHz.
  - 1050 MHz (descriptive): service time is 1.714 times the 1800 MHz time,
    matching the interpolated prediction. The fp16 decode knee therefore costs a
    compute-bound foreground about 71% longer service time.
  - Memory-bound frame: it barely reacts to the clock (ratio 1.011 at 900 MHz).
    W1 predicted 1.123, which is outside the 0.05 tolerance, so I'm reporting
    that as a miss.
  - HB2 with no background is supported. Deadline misses rise significantly at
    900 and 1200 MHz (Holm-adjusted p = 0.0029, 10 of 10 blocks) and do not at
    1500 MHz.
  - HB2 with the memory background is not supported, and it can't be tested as 
    designed. Every cell misses its deadline, including the 1800 MHz reference
    and unlocked, so time-slicing with the background dominates the clock
    effect.

  llama.cpp addendum (8e5b490 and a header-fix commit): the exclude line is
  removed and the file is registered without _DRAFT.

  I'm idle and executing nothing while it runs. Tell me when it's done.

  What the traces show:
  - Both processes appear. Each trace has two python CUDA contexts submitting
    3D-engine queue packets, and their packets are in flight at the same time.
    Which one is the foreground is my inference from packet volume, because the
    trace has no role label.
  - The traces can't show our context switches. With HAGS on, the DMA events
    aren't attributed to our contexts and the hardware-queue events only record
    queue creation.
  - No kernel records. The CUDA kernel table is missing because my fix kills the
    children, so CUPTI never flushes.
  - What fg_gpu_ms is. It is the time on the GPU timeline between two CUDA
    events on the foreground's stream. It includes any time the context is
    switched out between them, and it excludes any wait before the first event.
    That comes from the code, not the trace.
  - The 1800 MHz vs unlocked pattern is still unexplained (12.13 vs 12.10 ms at
    1800, 22.2 vs 8.86 ms unlocked). Co-run service times stay uninterpreted.
    The B report uses end-to-end latency, miss fraction and completed fraction
    only.
  - Trace files: the two .nsys-rep files (38 and 41 MB) are gitignored and stay
    at data/nsys/. The summary script output and status files are committed.

  Decisions:
  1. Kernel-side %globaltimer diagnostic to explain the fg_gpu_ms pattern? It
     needs an addendum before it runs. Recommendation: skip for now. A and A2
     matter more for the governor.
  2. After A: run A2 next, then I write the llama.cpp part L code and tests
     while nothing is running. Recommendation: yes.

  Reviewer checkpoint: due as a surprise. HAGS hides the context switches from
  the OS trace, and the fg_gpu_ms pattern is unexplained.

  Review findings 1–3 (response in reviews/2026-10-22_w2_A_A2_response.md,
  details in journal 0024)
  1. SA2 regressor: the reviewer was right. With each size's same-block alone
     service time, k = 0.983 (90% CI [0.974, 0.992]) and s0 = 0.243 ms, against
     0.395 as first run. So the "k far from 1" half of the globaltimer trigger
     was an artefact. My journal 0023 counted it as met, which was wrong.
     Journal 0024 corrects that, and 0023 is not edited.
  2. Capped-wait curve: partly confirmed. Against the unstretched slice end, the
     sparse bins and the 0.6 ms dip disappear. But a ripple remains: dips at
     0.35–0.40 and 0.80–0.85 ms, with a period of about 0.45 ms. tau moves to
     0.19 ms on that axis, against 0.26 on the observed-end axis. This is still
     the same data, so it is not independent.
  3. Thermal sensitivity for A:
     - HA1: unchanged.
     - HA2: NOT SUPPORTED in every version.
     - 3/6 to 1/6: this is lost power. With 6 block pairs the smallest possible
       Holm-adjusted p is 0.094, so nothing can reach significance. It is not a
       reversal, and the reviewer's "verdicts unchanged" holds.
     - A bug of mine: my first run printed "6/6 supported". A block missing one
       cell produced a NaN p-value, which Holm counted as significant. I fixed
       it test-first, and the registered all-cell result is unchanged.

  Globaltimer addendum
  - Status: drafted as
    experiments/w2_colocation_addendum_globaltimer_2026-10-22_DRAFT.md,
    uncommitted, with scope (a)–(c) as the reviewer proposed.

    fresh-seed replication of A's constant-slice cells plus instrumented 1 ms
    and 4 ms foreground cells.
  - Predictions: eight (P1–P8), written before any data, including q in 0.5–0.9
    ms, s in 0.2–0.3 ms, and no foreground/background block overlap.
  - Pre-checks: timer resolution, shared timebase and instrumentation overhead.
    If the overhead check fails, the timeline is not interpreted.

  - Code: I wrote the code, the HL1–HL3 analysis and 13 tests. A non-admin GPU

    minutes, but with a 25 s lead per cell the cell structure gives roughly 50

  - HL3 interpretation: HL3 compares token sequences over their shared prefix,
    since lengths differ with the clock. That is my reading of "identical", and
    I flagged it for the reviewer.
